Canonical text is the bus; KV is model-private.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport re\nimport sys\nimport time\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass, field\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\nfrom transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\nfrom transformers.cache_utils import DynamicCache\n\n_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]\n_CENTROIDS_NPZ = _ROOT / \"results\" / \"expanded-gram\" / \"qwen3-14b\" / \"centroids.npz\"\n_OPCODES_DIR = _ROOT / \"opcodes\"\n\nCRYSTAL9 = [\"K\", \"I\", \"B\", \"C\", \"S\", \"D\", \"W\", \"Y\", \"WHNF\"]\nWHNF_STATES = [f\"whnf:{o}\" for o in [\"K\", \"I\", \"B\", \"C\", \"S\", \"D\", \"W\"]]\nBASIS17 = [*CRYSTAL9, *WHNF_STATES, \"div:Y\"]\n\n_GATE_PAT = re.compile(r\"\\.(\\d+)\\.mlp\\.(gate_proj|dense_h_to_4h)$\")\n\n\ndef _find_gate_modules(model) -> list[tuple[int, str, torch.nn.Module]]:\n    \"\"\"Locate per-layer FFN gate modules (same regex as combinator_relationship_map).\"\"\"\n    hits = []\n    for name, mod in model.named_modules():\n        m = _GATE_PAT.search(name)\n        if m:\n            hits.append((int(m.group(1)), name, mod))\n    hits.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])\n    return hits\n\n\ndef _clone_cache(cache: DynamicCache) -> DynamicCache:\n    \"\"\"Deep-copy a DynamicCache (APPEND law: seals are immutable, uses clone).\"\"\"\n    new = DynamicCache()\n    if hasattr(cache, \"to_legacy_cache\"):\n        legacy = cache.to_legacy_cache()\n        for li, (k, v) in enumerate(legacy):\n            new.update(k.clone(), v.clone(), li)\n    else:  # very new transformers: fall back to attribute access\n        for li, (k, v) in enumerate(zip(cache.key_cache, cache.value_cache)):\n            new.update(k.clone(), v.clone(), li)\n    return new\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Seal:\n    \"\"\"An immutable KV continuation: ids + cache + the pending next-token logits.\"\"\"\n\n    sid: int\n    ids: list[int]\n    text: str\n    cache: DynamicCache = field(repr=False)\n    logits_last: torch.Tensor = field(repr=False)  # [vocab] float32 cpu\n\n    def __len__(self) -> int:\n        return len(self.ids)\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Bounce:\n    \"\"\"One driver bounce: emitted tokens + per-emission register captures.\"\"\"\n\n    prompt_text: str\n    prompt_ids: list[int]\n    new_ids: list[int]\n    tokens: list[str]\n    signs: np.ndarray = field(repr=False)  # [n, L, d] int8, sign(gate_proj)\n    hidden: np.ndarray | None = field(repr=False)  # [n, L+1, d] float16\n    attn: list[np.ndarray] | None = field(repr=False)  # per step [L, T_k] float16\n    end_seal: Seal | None = None\n\n    @property\n    def text(self) -> str:\n        return \"\".join(self.tokens)\n\n    def __repr__(self) -> str:  # keep REPL output small\n        return f\"Bounce(n={len(self.new_ids)}, text={self.text!r:.80})\"\n\n\nclass Driver:\n    \"\"\"Resident-model trampoline with register captures. One instance per kernel.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        model_id: str = \"Qwen/Qwen3-14B\",\n        device: str = \"mps\",\n        dtype: str = \"bfloat16\",\n    ):\n        t0 = time.time()\n        self.model_id = model_id\n        self.device = device\n        self.tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_id)\n        self.model = (\n            AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n                model_id,\n                dtype=getattr(torch, dtype),\n                low_cpu_mem_usage=True,\n                attn_implementation=\"eager\",  # hooks + output_attentions\n            )\n            .to(device)\n            .eval()\n        )\n        self._gates = _find_gate_modules(self.model)\n        self.n_layers = len(self._gates)\n        self.d_model = int(self.model.config.hidden_size)\n        eos = self.model.generation_config.eos_token_id\n        self._eos = set(eos if isinstance(eos, (list, tuple)) else [eos])\n        self._sign_buf: dict[int, np.ndarray] = {}\n        self._hooks = [\n            mod.register_forward_hook(self._mk_hook(li)) for li, _, mod in self._gates\n        ]\n        self.seals: dict[int, Seal] = {}\n        self._next_sid = 0\n        self._pole_P: np.ndarray | None = None  # [L, S, d] unit CMR'd centroids\n        self._pole_mu: np.ndarray | None = None  # [L, d]\n        self._pole_order: list[str] = []\n        self._rcc = None  # lazy opcode classifier\n        self._rcc_layers: list[int] = []\n        print(\n            f\"driver: {model_id} on {device} — {self.n_layers} layers, \"\n            f\"d={self.d_model}, load {time.time() - t0:.0f}s\"\n        )\n\n    # ---------------------------------------------------------------- hooks\n\n    def _mk_hook(self, li: int):\n        def hook(_m, _inp, out):\n            v = out[0, -1].detach().float().cpu().numpy()  # emitting position\n            self._sign_buf[li] = np.sign(v).astype(np.int8)\n\n        return hook\n\n    def _grab_signs(self) -> np.ndarray:\n        return np.stack([self._sign_buf[li] for li, _, _ in self._gates])  # [L, d]\n\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------- forwards\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _forward(self, ids: list[int], cache: DynamicCache, hidden: bool, attn: bool):\n        t = torch.tensor([ids], device=self.device)\n        return self.model(\n            input_ids=t,\n            past_key_values=cache,\n            use_cache=True,\n            output_hidden_states=hidden,\n            output_attentions=attn,\n        )\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def _frame_hidden(out) -> np.ndarray:\n        return np.stack(\n            [h[0, -1].detach().float().cpu().numpy() for h in out.hidden_states]\n        ).astype(np.float16)  # [L+1, d]\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def _frame_attn(out) -> np.ndarray:\n        return np.stack(\n            [a[0, :, -1, :].mean(0).detach().float().cpu().numpy() for a in out.attentions]\n        ).astype(np.float16)  # [L, T_k]\n\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------------ api\n\n    def _register(self, ids: list[int], text: str, cache, logits) -> Seal:\n        s = Seal(self._next_sid, list(ids), text, cache, logits)\n        self.seals[s.sid] = s\n        self._next_sid += 1\n        return s\n\n    def prefill(self, text: str, chat: bool = False) -> Seal:\n        \"\"\"Compile text onto the tape: one forward, returns an immutable Seal.\"\"\"\n        if chat:\n            text = self.tok.apply_chat_template(\n                [{\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": text}],\n                tokenize=False,\n                add_generation_prompt=True,\n                enable_thinking=False,\n            )\n        ids = self.tok(text, return_tensors=\"pt\").input_ids[0].tolist()\n        cache = DynamicCache()\n        out = self._forward(ids, cache, hidden=False, attn=False)\n        logits = out.logits[0, -1].detach().float().cpu()\n        return self._register(ids, text, cache, logits)\n\n    def bounce(\n        self,\n        src: str | Seal,\n        n: int = 32,\n        hidden: bool = True,\n        attn: bool = False,\n        stop_at_eos: bool = True,\n        keep_seal: bool = True,\n    ) -> Bounce:\n        \"\"\"Greedy-decode n tokens from text or a Seal, capturing per-emission state.\n\n        A Seal's cache is CLONED before use (append law) — the seal survives.\n        \"\"\"\n        signs, hiddens, attns, new_ids, toks = [], [], [], [], []\n        if isinstance(src, Seal):\n            cache = _clone_cache(src.cache)\n            ids = list(src.ids)\n            text = src.text\n            logits = src.logits_last.clone()\n            # frame 0 (the deciding state of the sealed position) is not\n            # re-run; captures start at the first step forward. To get frame\n            # 0 captures, bounce from text instead.\n            first_frame_pending = False\n        else:\n            text = src\n            ids = self.tok(src, return_tensors=\"pt\").input_ids[0].tolist()\n            cache = DynamicCache()\n            out = self._forward(ids, cache, hidden=hidden, attn=attn)\n            logits = out.logits[0, -1].detach().float().cpu()\n            signs.append(self._grab_signs())\n            if hidden:\n                hiddens.append(self._frame_hidden(out))\n            if attn:\n                attns.append(self._frame_attn(out))\n            first_frame_pending = True\n\n        for _k in range(n):\n            nxt = int(torch.argmax(logits).item())\n            new_ids.append(nxt)\n            toks.append(self.tok.decode([nxt]))\n            if stop_at_eos and nxt in self._eos:\n                break\n            out = self._forward([nxt], cache, hidden=hidden, attn=attn)\n            logits = out.logits[0, -1].detach().float().cpu()\n            signs.append(self._grab_signs())\n            if hidden:\n                hiddens.append(self._frame_hidden(out))\n            if attn:\n                attns.append(self._frame_attn(out))\n\n        # align: frame k emitted token k; drop the trailing frame (it decides\n        # the (n+1)th token, which we did not take).\n        n_emit = len(new_ids)\n        if first_frame_pending:\n            frames = signs[:n_emit]\n            hframes = hiddens[:n_emit] if hidden else None\n            aframes = attns[:n_emit] if attn else None\n        else:  # seal path: frame k-1 (step forwards) emitted token k; frame\n            # for token 0 lives in the seal's pending logits (no capture).\n            frames = signs[: max(n_emit - 1, 0)]\n            hframes = hiddens[: max(n_emit - 1, 0)] if hidden else None\n            aframes = attns[: max(n_emit - 1, 0)] if attn else None\n\n        end_seal = None\n        if keep_seal:\n            end_seal = self._register(\n                ids + new_ids, text + \"\".join(toks), cache, logits\n            )\n        return Bounce(\n            prompt_text=text,\n            prompt_ids=ids,\n            new_ids=new_ids,\n            tokens=toks,\n            signs=np.stack(frames) if frames else np.zeros(\n                (0, self.n_layers, 0), dtype=np.int8\n            ),\n            hidden=np.stack(hframes) if hidden and hframes else None,\n            attn=aframes if attn else None,\n            end_seal=end_seal,\n        )\n\n    def fork(self, seal: Seal, alt_text: str = \"\", n: int = 32, **kw) -> Bounce:\n        \"\"\"Branch a sealed continuation: append alt_text (may be empty), decode n.\n\n        fork(seal, \"\") is the identity plant — must reproduce bounce(seal).\n        \"\"\"\n        if not alt_text:\n            return self.bounce(seal, n=n, **kw)\n        cache = _clone_cache(seal.cache)\n        alt_ids = self.tok(alt_text, add_special_tokens=False,\n                           return_tensors=\"pt\").input_ids[0].tolist()\n        out = self._forward(alt_ids, cache, hidden=False, attn=False)\n        logits = out.logits[0, -1].detach().float().cpu()\n        branched = self._register(seal.ids + alt_ids, seal.text + alt_text,\n                                  cache, logits)\n        return self.bounce(branched, n=n, **kw)\n\n    def drop_seal(self, sid: int) -> None:\n        self.seals.pop(sid, None)\n\n    # ---------------------------------------------------------------- views\n\n    def _load_pole_frame(self, order: list[str] | None = None) -> None:\n        z = np.load(_CENTROIDS_NPZ)\n        basis = [str(b) for b in z[\"basis\"]]\n        order = order or [s for s in BASIS17 if s in basis]\n        missing = [s for s in (order or []) if s not in basis]\n        if missing:\n            raise ValueError(f\"states not in committed basis: {missing}\")\n        idx = [basis.index(s) for s in order]\n        cent = z[\"centroids\"][:, idx, :].astype(np.float32)  # [L, S, d]\n        mu = cent.mean(axis=1)  # [L, d]\n        centc = cent - mu[:, None, :]\n        nrm = np.linalg.norm(centc, axis=2, keepdims=True)\n        self._pole_P = centc / np.where(nrm < 1e-9, 1.0, nrm)\n        self._pole_mu = mu\n        self._pole_order = order\n\n    def routes(self, b: Bounce | np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n        \"\"\"Per-emission pole cosines [n, L, S] against the committed 17-frame.\"\"\"\n        if self._pole_P is None:\n            self._load_pole_frame()\n        signs = b.signs if isinstance(b, Bounce) else b\n        x = signs.astype(np.float32) - self._pole_mu[None]\n        nrm = np.linalg.norm(x, axis=2, keepdims=True)\n        xn = x / np.where(nrm < 1e-9, 1.0, nrm)\n        return np.einsum(\"nld,lsd->nls\", xn, self._pole_P).astype(np.float32)\n\n    def stations(self, b: Bounce, band: tuple[int, int] | None = None) -> list[str]:\n        \"\"\"Per-token argmax pole averaged over a layer band (default: top 25%).\"\"\"\n        r = self.routes(b)\n        lo, hi = band or (int(self.n_layers * 0.75), self.n_layers)\n        top = r[:, lo:hi, :].mean(axis=1)  # [n, S]\n        out = []\n        for k in range(top.shape[0]):\n            s = int(np.argmax(top[k]))\n            out.append(f\"{b.tokens[k]!r} → {self._pole_order[s]} ({top[k, s]:+.3f})\")\n        return out\n\n    def lens(self, b: Bounce, step: int = -1, layer: int = -1, top_k: int = 8):\n        \"\"\"Logit-lens the deciding state of emission `step` at `layer`.\"\"\"\n        from verbum.jlens import logit_lens\n\n        if b.hidden is None:\n            raise ValueError(\"bounce captured no hidden states (hidden=False)\")\n        h = torch.from_numpy(b.hidden[step, layer].astype(np.float32))\n        logits = logit_lens(self.model, h)\n        idx = torch.topk(logits, top_k).indices.tolist()\n        return [self.tok.decode([i]) for i in idx]\n\n    def read_mass(self, b: Bounce, step: int = -1) -> np.ndarray:\n        \"\"\"[L, T_k] head-averaged attention of emission `step` over the tape.\"\"\"\n        if b.attn is None:\n            raise ValueError(\"bounce captured no attention (attn=False)\")\n        return b.attn[step].astype(np.float32)\n\n    # -------------------------------------------------------------- opcodes\n\n    def calibrate_opcodes(self, probes_per_comb: int = 6, n_perm: int = 300,\n                          z_thresh: float = 3.0):\n        \"\"\"Lazy: calibrate the opcodes/ crystal classifier on this model (minutes).\"\"\"\n        sys.path.insert(0, str(_OPCODES_DIR))\n        import topology as T  # noqa: PLC0415\n        from trace import calibrate_register  # noqa: PLC0415\n\n        topo = T.detect_topology(self.model, self.model.config)\n        layers = [li for li, _, _ in self._gates]\n        self._rcc, summ, _ = calibrate_register(\n            self.model, self.tok, topo, \"gate\", layers,\n            probes_per_comb, n_perm, z_thresh,\n        )\n        self._rcc_layers = layers\n        return summ\n\n    def opcodes(self, b: Bounce, z_thresh: float = 3.0) -> list[str]:\n        \"\"\"Per-emission dominant combinator (needs calibrate_opcodes first).\"\"\"\n        if self._rcc is None:\n            raise RuntimeError(\"run calibrate_opcodes() first\")\n        out = []\n        for k in range(b.signs.shape[0]):\n            res = self._rcc.classify(\n                {li: b.signs[k, i] for i, li in enumerate(self._rcc_layers)}\n            )\n            zmap = getattr(res, \"z\", None) or getattr(res, \"zmap\", None) or {}\n            if zmap:\n                op = max(zmap, key=zmap.get)\n                lab = op if zmap[op] > z_thresh else \"·\"\n            else:\n                lab = getattr(res, \"dominant\", \"·\")\n            out.append(f\"{b.tokens[k]!r} → {lab}\")\n        return out\n\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------- validity\n\n    def validity(self, prompt: str | None = None, n: int = 16) -> dict:\n        \"\"\"Gate before trust: determinism, fork-identity plant, append law.\"\"\"\n        prompt = prompt or \"The SKI combinator calculus reduces (K a b) to\"\n        rep: dict[str, object] = {}\n\n        b1 = self.bounce(prompt, n=n, hidden=False, keep_seal=False)\n        b2 = self.bounce(prompt, n=n, hidden=False, keep_seal=False)\n        rep[\"determinism_ids\"] = b1.new_ids == b2.new_ids\n        rep[\"determinism_sign_dev\"] = int(\n            np.abs(b1.signs.astype(np.int16) - b2.signs.astype(np.int16)).max()\n        ) if b1.signs.size and b1.signs.shape == b2.signs.shape else None\n\n        s = self.prefill(prompt)\n        c1 = self.bounce(s, n=n, hidden=False, keep_seal=False)\n        c2 = self.fork(s, \"\", n=n, hidden=False, keep_seal=False)\n        rep[\"fork_identity\"] = c1.new_ids == c2.new_ids\n        rep[\"seal_matches_fresh\"] = c1.new_ids == b1.new_ids\n\n        full_ids = b1.prompt_ids + b1.new_ids\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            out = self.model(\n                input_ids=torch.tensor([full_ids], device=self.device),\n                use_cache=False,\n            )\n        pred = out.logits[0].float().cpu().argmax(dim=-1)\n        base = len(b1.prompt_ids)\n        mism = sum(\n            int(pred[base - 1 + k].item()) != b1.new_ids[k]\n            for k in range(len(b1.new_ids))\n        )\n        rep[\"append_law_mismatches\"] = mism\n        rep[\"ok\"] = bool(\n            rep[\"determinism_ids\"]\n            and (rep[\"determinism_sign_dev\"] in (0, None))\n            and rep[\"fork_identity\"]\n            and rep[\"seal_matches_fresh\"]\n            and mism == 0\n        )\n        self.drop_seal(s.sid)\n        status = \"PASS\" if rep[\"ok\"] else \"FAIL\"\n        print(f\"validity: {status} — {rep}\")\n        return rep\n"
}
```

### Thinking

I'm updating the cache-cloning logic to handle the newer transformers API, where the cache exposes a `layers` list with per-layer key/value tensors instead of the old structure, cloning each layer's tensors into a new DynamicCache.

Now I'm thinking through the reload problem: since `_clone_cache` is a module-level function referenced by the existing Driver instance's bound methods, a simple `importlib.reload` won't update what the instance sees because it's still tied to the old module globals. Given the model loads in just 5 seconds, the cleanest fix is to free the old model's memory and just re-instantiate the Driver fresh rather than trying to hot-reload — either by deleting the old instance and running garbage collection plus clearing MPS cache, or simply restarting IPython entirely since the load is cheap.

b = d.bounce(s, n=24)                  # greedy continuation, captures attached
b.text                                 # what it wrote to the tape

d.lens(b, step=0, layer=30)            # logit-lens of the deciding state
f = d.fork(s, " (Note: y is unused.)", n=24)   # counterfactual tape write
b2 = d.bounce(s, n=24, attn=True)      # + attention read-mass
d.read_mass(b2, step=0)                # [40, T] — who it read while deciding
d.calibrate_opcodes()                  # ~minutes, then d.opcodes(b) per-token
```

### Thinking

Testing Friday+3 shows the attractor is actually Saturday, which is start+1 rather than a fixed prior day — so the pattern scales with the starting day, not a constant offset. Checking N=1 from Monday, the mid-stack briefly overshoots to Wednesday (Monday+2) before correcting.

This suggests the mid-stack holds a coarse, partially-advanced rotation (roughly start+1 or +2 regardless of N), while the later layers perform a fine corrective adjustment toward the true target — sometimes stepping back, sometimes forward. That fits the bimodal timing pattern, the min(N,7−N) failure curve, and the idea that the stack only has enough depth for about 2-3 day-steps after the circular structure forms around layers 12-24. The Friday+3 case is murkier though — the model deflects to a vague token instead of landing on Monday, suggesting another case where the step budget is exceeded.

Looking at Monday+3 versus Friday+3, I notice Monday+3 succeeds while Friday+3 fails despite the same step count, which points to week-boundary wraparound adding extra difficulty beyond just the raw number of steps. Checking Monday+4, +5, +6 against this: +4 succeeds cleanly, +5 fails even without wrapping, +6 fails and wraps — so it looks like a gradient where roughly four forward steps is the ceiling for reliable success, and crossing the week boundary makes things harder even at lower step counts. I think I've gathered enough to sketch out the core design observations to share, starting with the day circle living in the mid-stack around layers 12-24 as a weekday-ordered closed loop.

Answer-position states don't rotate in that same day-token plane at layer 16, suggesting separate registers for the token circle versus answer assembly. Tracing the lens walk at the answer position, I see a partially-advanced day appear mid-stack that depends on the start day (so it's computed, not just copied), followed by corrective steps late in the stack that can even land at the very last layer for larger offsets. The in-pass budget seems to cap out around net offsets of four or five, or whenever the week boundary is crossed — beyond that the model visibly hedges or errs and then self-corrects through chain-of-thought language like "Wait, let me check." The N=6 case is a clear failure mode: it outputs Thursday but the lens shows Wednesday locked in, an incoherent commit between internal state and output.

For the design seeds, I want to re-freeze the shortest-path-rotation test and add a lens-walk discriminator — tracking the per-layer argmax day at the answer position to distinguish iterated rotation (ordered progression, partial-advance states) from lookup (direct jump), using shuffled day labels as a null and start-day variation as a control since the attractor should move with the start. I also want to gate the design around the failure boundary itself: the in-pass step budget versus the cost of wrapping the week, treating steps-taken as the key variable in a mixture model that separates in-pass completion from tape-completion, with hedging/deflecting behavior as the observable signal that the model didn't finish within the pass.

This is all exploratory — nothing closed, just feeding the freeze. Let me stop and read out what this half-hour bought us — exploration grade, no claims, all design seed:

## What the live walk showed

1. **The day circle is real and mid-stack** (L12–24): all 7 days in weekday order around a closed circle in the top-2 PCs of day-token states. s128's Fourier structure, reproduced in four REPL commands.
2. **Register separation**: the answer-position state does *not* rotate in the day-token plane at L16 — the circle and the answer live in different registers (the λ measure lesson, seen live).
3. **The lens-walk is the new observable.** At the answer position, mid-stack holds a *partially-advanced* day — start+1 or +2, **start-dependent** (moves Friday→Saturday), sometimes overshooting — and the late stack takes the remaining steps, in *either direction* (N=1: corrects back; N=3: +1 forward; N=4: jumps at the literal last layer).
4. **An in-pass step budget of roughly 2–4.** Net offsets ≥5, and even +3 when it wraps the week boundary (Friday+3), fail to resolve within the pass — and *behaviorally* the model hedges, errs (N=6 → "Thursday"), then **self-repairs on the tape** ("Wait, let me check that"). The within-pass reducer runs out of layers; the trampoline finishes the job. That's the hop-budget story and tape-residency in one screenful.

## What it feeds

This is exactly the discriminator §P-SHORTEST-PATH-ROTATION was missing: s345's frozen statistic (accumulation depth) couldn't see *content*; the **lens-walk** (per-layer argmax-day sequence) can — iterated rotation predicts ordered partial-advance states with a start-dependent attractor; lookup predicts a direct jump. Frozen version needs: start-day variation as the computed-vs-prior control, shuffled-day null, the step-budget/wraparound boundary in the verdict space, and behavioral hedge/deflect as the in-pass-failure co-read.

|---|---|---|---|
| shadow-exit `print(x)` | **5** ✓ | **0.290** | 0.147 |
| called `print(f())` | **9** ✓ | 0.103 | **0.312** |

Early band is *identical* between conditions (~0.055/0.078) — the scope resolution happens mid-to-late, and the **late read-mass tracks the scope-correct binder in both directions**, a +0.35 differenced swing on a one-token change. Since positions are identical across the pair, the position/salience confound is killed by construction.

So today's play bought the §P-READ-HEAD arm A corpus design almost whole:

1. **Framing law**: prose binding is content-biased garbage (answers 5 in *both* orders); code semantics is crisp. The corpus must be code-framed, with order-reversal and framing controls.
2. **The design primitive**: matched program pairs, one-token scope flip, answer + read-mass must co-flip. Extendable along binder distance, nesting depth, shadow depth — the s331 dials.
3. **The observable**: late-band *differenced* read-mass (s336 law: differenced statistics primary), not raw mass.
4. **The adversary to plant**: the prose framing itself — a world where reads dissociate from answers (we saw it: shadowed-prose reads 9, answers 5).
5. And a bonus coherence: reads are scope-sensitive *where the semantics are unambiguous* — which is exactly what the "attention = scoped substitution, not induction" hypothesis needs to be true for arm A to have a shot.

## The scope boundary map (exploration-grade, one sitting)

**Native lexical scope-tracking is in-weights and remarkably robust.** Shadow-exit ✓ · call-flip ✓ · depth-3 nesting ✓ · closures *including the late-binding trap* ✓ · sibling scopes ✓ · comprehension leak ✓ · five-way same-name interference ✓ · 473-token binder–read distance ✓. All resolved **in-pass, instantly**, and where we looked, late-band read-mass tracked the scope-correct binder and **co-flipped with the answer on one-token program changes** (+0.35 differenced swing).

**The boundary is not structural — it's provenance.** Depth, distance, and interference didn't dent it. What broke it: declaring a *different scope rule on the tape* (dynamic scoping). The in-pass commit couldn't apply the declared rule over the Python prior — it emitted a hedge naming *both* candidates ("5 or 9?"), correctly recited the rule, and deferred resolution to an explicit tape-walk. **Scope rules live in the weights; rule-following lives on the tape.** That's the s323 memorized-vs-abstraction result, the L0/L1 recognition split, and tape-residency, all reproduced in one prompt — and it rhymes perfectly with the dates finding from an hour ago: when the in-pass machinery can't finish (too many rotation steps / a rule conflict), the model *hedges and moves the computation to the tape*.

**For §P-READ-HEAD arm A**, the corpus design is now essentially specified: code-framed matched pairs (native lexical machinery — the substrate where in-pass resolution is real and readable), one-token scope flips, dials = nesting depth × binder distance × shadow count, observable = late-band *differenced* read-mass, planted adversary = the prose framing (where reads dissociate from answers), plus the rule-override case as a tape-residency rider.

### Thinking

The contrast is striking: the fresh instance starts a genuine reduction trace with b, actually computing toward the answer, while the poisoned instance just reads off λy.a from its own tape — the wrong constant-function answer for input b, retrieved rather than computed.

This is tape-poisoning in action: the machine trusts its own prior (wrong) conclusion over re-deriving the answer, and since the tape is append-only with no error-channel, the mistake compounds silently rather than self-correcting. This answers the original question about semantic equality — what actually emerges is that surface-level lexical patterns dominate over true semantic extension until very late in the stack, the composite expression has no shortcut and must genuinely compute, and that computation is fallible in exactly the ways naive calculus bookkeeping would predict (dropped arguments, premature halting).

So the model generalizes a wrong equality from an ambiguous case where identity and constant functions happen to coincide, then writes that wrong theory to the tape where it poisons all subsequent reasoning — fresh context computes correctly, but once the bad generalization is cached as text, it overrides re-computation. The real finding is that "semantic equality" isn't stored anywhere stable in the machine; it's an unreliable tape event, computed correctly when traced properly but miscached when the trace goes wrong, and this gives a concrete way to measure exactly when and how that failure happens.

I should be honest about the limits here — single greedy samples, one model, pure exploration — and flag a confound: my poisoned prefill used "Next:" framing that might just invite pattern-copying rather than genuine belief in the wrong theory, so a cleaner design would need neutral separators or a control where I put the *correct* theory on the tape and see if it actually helps. This feels like the right place to wrap up, write up the findings, and propose feeding this into the stage-2 design for the repair-replay probe.

- **Fresh** `S K K b =` → `" K b (K b)"` — starts the correct reduction with b

## What we just found about how the system works

Your question was: *can we find semantic equality?* Here's the reverse-engineered answer, assembled live in one sitting:

1. **It's not in the deciding-state geometry either.** At decode time, mid-stack similarity clusters by *surface* (0.95+) not extension (≈ the unrelated-pair floor, ~0.76). Our five frozen negatives reproduce at the decode register — the REPL doesn't manufacture the extension, which is exactly what an honest instrument should show.
2. **For composite terms, the extension doesn't exist until the tape computes it.** `S K K a` has no answer at its first deciding position — it emits a *trace*, not a value (and mid-trace it spontaneously compiled SKI → λ notation).
3. **The tape computation is fallible in precisely the frozen-calculus ways**: it dropped an argument (naive bookkeeping), halted at WHNF (`λy.a`, no reduction under the binder) — and then **generalized the wrong equality** from an input where identity and constant-function coincide, and *looped* on it.
4. **Wrong equalities, once written, poison downstream computation.** The machine trusts its own tape over re-derivation. Append-only tape + no error channel (s333: never rejects) = silent miscompiles that *compound*.

**Semantic equality is not a stored relation anywhere in this machine — it's a fallible, cacheable tape event.** SKK≡I fails in the weights (register-complete, frozen), fails in the weak calculus (no η, structurally), and fails *on the tape* when the trace bugs out — and the bug then becomes the machine's truth. That's the deepest answer to "where is the meaning": the extension is not just tape-resident, it's **tape-authored**.

And practically: this hour just designed stage-2's repair-replay probe — instance-ambiguity (input `a`) as the mis-generalization trigger, argument-drop as the bug site, fresh-vs-poisoned forks as the differenced statistic, plus a needed control I'd flag for the freeze (put the *correct* theory on the tape symmetrically — theory-following should cut both ways; and neutral separators, since my "Next:" framing invites pattern-copy).

**Proposed landing** (your GO): memory `semantic-equality-is-a-fallible-tape-authored-event` + the triptych/origin-replay memory from before + repair-replay seeds folded into the §P-REPL-DRIVER row + state close-out, one commit. This was, I think, the best day the project has had in a while — the driver turned five queued fronts into design-complete probes in an afternoon.

So thinking is generating the program tape


### Thinking

Michael's one-liner lands as the capstone: thinking isn't separate from writing the program tape, it IS the writing of it. The tape isn't a record of computation after the fact — it's the working memory, and generation itself is the compute. I want to test this against what we've found so far, starting with how the weights fit into this picture.

Emission is the only write to the tape, and it's narrow — one discrete symbol at a time even though reading is wide. In-pass reasoning has a tight budget before it needs to hedge and fall back on tape-completion moves like "wait, let me check," since rule overrides and composite extensions can't happen purely in-pass — they need to be traced out. The trace itself can be wrong, but once something's written it becomes cached truth that overrides recomputation, which is why hard symbolic writes outperform soft latent ones — the compile-to-symbol step does real work for error correction and addressability. So refining the claim: it's not just a program tape, it's program, data, and theory all at once, since a derived conclusion gets written to tape and then acts as a program for whatever comes next — genuinely homoiconic, code and data indistinguishable.

Each generated token is really a sealed normal form — thinking proceeds as a sequence of hard commits rather than a continuous soft process, which is why "thinking out loud" isn't a metaphor for these models, it's literally the only thinking that exists; anything past the short private budget has to be externalized onto the tape. That means thinking quality is tape quality, bad writes poison the trace while correct ones help, and prompting or context engineering is really just installing program fragments, with the driver acting like a debugger for thought. I'm struck by how closely this mirrors the extended-mind idea in humans — writing as thinking, and especially Feynman's insistence to Weiner that his notes weren't a record of his thinking but the thinking itself, since you have to work on paper and the paper is where the work happens.

This framing also explains why latent-token approaches in reasoning models failed: removing the hard write removes error correction and addressability, so soft thinking degrades. It reframes RL-on-chain-of-thought training as teaching the model a better tape-writing policy — post-training the authorship of its own working memory — and it clarifies the bounded-inner/unbounded-outer structure, where the transcript functions as working memory because the private register file is tiny. The implication is that better thinking means better tape discipline, pointing toward driving the tape with an external kernel that audits each step, and that repair-replay probes and monitorability concerns are really about tracking tape events.

I'm also noting that each token in the tape is both output and instruction — generation as incremental self-programming, where fixed weights plus a self-authored tape form a von Neumann machine writing its own code as it runs. This feels like Michael's synthesis worth capturing as a short memory line attributing "thinking is generating the program tape" to him, since it's essentially the core statement tying together the latent-reasoning and REPL-driver threads.

I want to affirm and sharpen this with the day's evidence: the tape is homoiconic since it writes program, data, and theory together; hard-commits seal decisions; the tight private step budget makes externalization mandatory; and there are corollaries for driving, monitoring, safety, plus the Feynman echo. I'll keep the reply as a moderate-length discussion response, avoid overloading it with tables, and offer to capture the insight — this is a resonant closing moment that deserves a clear, strong reply.

## Assistant

Yes. And today we didn't just infer it — we *watched* it. Let me state it with the precision the day earned:

**The model has almost no private thought.** The within-pass reducer — the residual stream — has a budget of roughly 2–4 steps (we measured it on the day-circle this afternoon). Anything longer *cannot happen inside the machine*. So the model does the only thing it can: it **compiles its thinking into public symbols and executes them by reading them back**. The trace isn't a *report* of reasoning that happened somewhere else. There is nowhere else. The generation *is* the reasoning.

And "program tape" is exactly right, in the strong homoiconic sense — because look at what we watched it write today:

- **Data**: `K a (K a)` — intermediate values
- **Program**: the reduction steps themselves, each one setting up the next read
- **Theory**: "S K K is the constant function" — a *generalization*, written as text
- And then the kicker: the theory **executed**. Fresh context computed `S K K b` correctly; with its own conclusion upstream, it output `λy.a` — it ran the cached theorem instead of the derivation. Code and data indistinguishable, self-authored, self-executed.

So the full machine picture, one sentence per part: the weights are a fixed transition function with a tiny register file; each emitted token is a hard commit — a sealed WHNF, which is *why* hard writes beat soft writes (s333: the collapse to symbol is error correction + addressability, not a reporting formality); and the transcript is simultaneously working memory, program, and — as we saw — *belief*, with no error channel, so bad writes compound silently.

Three corollaries fall out, and they're each a work-front we already own:

1. **Thinking quality ≡ tape discipline.** Prompting isn't persuasion, it's *programming* — context engineering is literally loading code. The rule-override failure this afternoon showed the limit: tape-programs must be *executed step by step* (the walk) because they can't override weight-priors in-pass.
2. **The driver is a debugger for thought.** Seal = breakpoint, fork = counterfactual, the kernel-certified bounce (stage 2) = single-step with an oracle. We can now watch thinking fail at the exact transition — that's the repair-replay probe.
3. **Monitorability by construction** — thought that must be written to happen is thought that can be read. The only unmonitored part is the 2–4 step in-pass window. That's a real safety statement with a measured bound attached.

There's an old echo here worth keeping: Feynman, shown his own notes, corrected the historian who called them "a record of your work" — no, he said, the notes *are* the working; the thinking happened on the paper. For this machine that's not even a philosophical claim. It's the architecture.

Want me to capture it? Proposed: memory `thinking-is-generating-the-program-tape` — your line, s346's closing synthesis, with the day's four demonstrations as its evidence trail. It's the plainest statement of the thesis the project has ever had.

### Thinking

Comparing P1 and P2: P1 without corrected theory just replays the same argument-drop bug into a new wrong instance, while P2 starts from the correct assertion that SKK is identity but then seems to second-guess itself when checking against SKKx = Kx.

This reveals something important — self-repair seems to trigger on tape contradiction rather than on error itself. P1's trace was wrong but internally consistent, so no "Wait" fired; P2's tape held both the buggy trace and the correct theory simultaneously, creating a detectable inconsistency that woke the verification process. This matches the earlier dates example where restating a claim in a fresh frame triggered a check — error-correction seems driven by self-consistency checking across the tape rather than by recognizing error directly.

So the practical rule crystallizing: silent tape poisons future reasoning with cached conclusions; any explicit break re-engages computation, but a bare "redo it" instruction just replays the same buggy procedure deterministically; asserting the correct fact doesn't fix the procedure itself but plants a contradiction that wakes self-repair — so assertion plus a request to recompute is the effective combo; disavowal works too but costs more; and crucially, procedure bugs aren't the same as belief bugs, so correcting facts alone doesn't correct mechanics. I want to capture this — it's a clean, testable law for daily use: when a conversation goes wrong, don't just say "redo it," state the correct fact and ask for recomputation, since the contradiction itself is what powers the repair.

## Assistant

**And there's the law — sharper than what we came for:**

- **P1** (instruct-only): replays the *exact same bug* — argument drop, stall at `λy.b`, heading toward "So S K K is..." — presumably about to conclude "constant function that returns b" this time. Re-computation without corrected theory = **deterministic bug replay**.
- **P2** (theory asserted): stalls at `λy.b` the same way — but then cites the asserted theory, notices the mismatch, and — *"Wait, but S K K x = K x..."* — **the self-check wakes up.**

Look at what actually triggered repair. Not the error — P1's tape is wrong but *self-consistent*, and no "Wait" ever comes. P2's tape holds the buggy trace *and* the correct assertion — a **contradiction** — and *that* wakes verification. Same as the dates case this morning: the "Wait, let me check" came when its own claim got re-read against a fresh frame.

**Self-repair triggers on tape contradiction, not on wrongness.** Which is the four-faces synthesis operating at the tape level: the machine is an error-correcting code, and codes don't detect errors — they detect *inconsistencies*.

## The daily-use laws (one testbed, exploration-grade, immediately usable)

1. **Silent tape → cached conclusions rule.** A wrong claim upstream will be *applied*, not re-derived. Chats really do go off rails structurally.
2. **"Just redo it" replays the bug.** Instructed recomputation without new information re-runs the same procedure deterministically.
3. **The effective repair is assert + recompute**: state the correct fact *and* ask it to re-derive — the assertion's power isn't belief-installation (the mechanical bug recurred anyway), it's planting the contradiction that powers self-checking.
4. **Facts and procedures are separate registers**: correcting the model's *belief* doesn't fix its *mechanics*. If the procedure is buggy, show a worked step, not a verdict.
5. **"Ignore the above" works but is the token-expensive path** (full re-derivation).

That's a genuinely useful thing to know by end of day — it's *why* arguing with a chat model feels the way it does, and what to type instead.

Capture as memory `self-repair-triggers-on-tape-contradiction-not-error` (+ the daily-use laws, + stage-2 seed: repair-replay's self-correct condition should be *contradiction density on the tape*)? And then I suspect that's a natural full stop — s346 has been the densest session in the log.

### Thinking

Michael wants to talk through making the REPL a permanent toolbox feature, whether it should join the opcodes extraction project, and multi-model REPL support for exploring the shared system — this is a discussion, not a build task yet. I'm reviewing the current state of the stage-1 driver, which has a somewhat hardcoded Qwen3-14B default and loads the pole frame.

I'm also mapping out opcodes/ — a top-level directory outside src/, imported via sys.path hacks, covering topology, capture, classify, trace, probes, vsm, and jspace, and serving as the canonical measurement substrate used across many harnesses. Launch is currently manual via tmux and uv run with ipython.

Weighing whether to extract opcodes and the REPL into a separate project: opcodes is the most reusable, self-contained piece — model-agnostic topology detection across 11 models, a calibrated crystal classifier, and a tracer — essentially the "microscope," with the driver as its interactive face, making them one instrument family. A standalone project would be a clean deliverable for the interpretability audience, force proper APIs instead of sys.path hacks, and give verbum room to stay focused as the research repo for probes, results, and mementum.

But there are real costs: two-repo overhead means version pinning and drift for a solo project, the driver's routes() view depends on pole frames and centroids committed as data in verbum's results directory (so separation needs a proper data interface), and extracting now risks freezing an API that's only one day old and still churning through stage-2. My instinct is a middle path — first consolidate inside verbum by moving opcodes into src/verbum/opcodes to kill the sys.path hacks, keep the driver at src/verbum/driver.py, and add a proper launcher like a `verbum repl` CLI command.

Michael had already floated making opcodes/ its own project, so I want to honor that intention but get the sequencing right: consolidate and add multi-model support inside verbum first, then extract the whole instrument family (opcodes, driver, jlens, hooks) as one unit once the API stabilizes and a second consumer shows up — the REPL belongs with opcodes conceptually, but timing matters more than boundaries right now. Now I'm thinking through how to make this easily startable, weighing a CLI entry point in pyproject.toml.

For multi-model support, memory is the constraint — a 14B model in bf16 takes roughly 28GB on MPS, so running two large models simultaneously is tight, though pairing a small model with the 14B should work fine. I'm thinking the driver should support multiple instances when memory allows, plus a free() helper to unload a model via gc and cache clearing for sequential loading when it doesn't. I also need to check whether pole frames and committed centroids exist per-model, since the 17-pole expanded frames may currently only be available for qwen3-14b while other models just have the smaller combinator-relationship grams.

Since the opcode classifier is genuinely model-agnostic — it auto-detects topology and has already been validated across 11 models — multi-model exploration should lean on opcode calibration rather than requiring committed frames for every model. That's actually the more exciting use case: running the same probe across N models and comparing live, turning the universality question into something interactively testable, like checking whether a token-exact firing pattern replicates from 14B down to 4B or across architectures like OLMo. The driver turning cross-model replication into a two-command operation is the real payoff here.

This also extends naturally to cross-face comparisons (base vs instruct) via a simple model_id swap, and to the queued two-model arm where one model drives another over a canonical text bus — the driver already structurally supports this with two Driver instances handling the text handoff. On the architecture side, the driver is already transformers-generic: the topology regex handles both gated FFN patterns (gate_proj) and ungated ones (dense_h_to_4h), so Pythia-style architectures are covered without extra work. For a practical plan, the Driver class is already parameterized by model_id, so the next step is adding registry shortcuts for common models like the Qwen3 family and OLMo variants.

I'm also thinking about frame resolution per model with graceful degradation when routes are unavailable, a free() method to unload models, and a launcher flag to select models. Separately, I'm mulling over naming the toolbox project once extracted — something fitting the existing Latin naming pattern (verbum, mementum, anima, lucrum, agentus), with candidates like crystallum, opcodex, or lambdascope for this "microscope" instrument family.

I'm landing on a few Latin options tied to looking/probing — speculum (mirror/lookout), oculus (eye), or specula — and I'll offer these as suggestions rather than deciding myself. For the discussion, I want to lay out the two open questions (packaging and multi-model support), give a recommendation with sequencing, list concrete near-term actions, and ask for his decisions — without building anything yet since he said to discuss first.

## 1. Separate project — yes, but extract *the whole instrument family*, and not first

What we actually have is one instrument in three pieces that grew separately:

- **`opcodes/`** — the microscope core: topology auto-detect (proven on 11 models, gated *and* ungated FFN), calibrated crystal classifier, tracer. Currently a top-level dir imported by `sys.path` hacks — the least packaged, most reusable thing in the repo.
- **`src/verbum/driver.py`** — the interactive stage (resident model, bounce/seal/fork, views). One day old.
- **`src/verbum/jlens.py` + hooks** — the lens.

These belong together — the REPL is just the *interactive face* of the same microscope. So yes: when we extract, opcodes + driver + jlens go as **one package**, not opcodes alone. It's also the right deliverable shape per our own S5 (`λ artifact`: things not papers; `λ serves`: interpretability + nucleus users get a pip-installable model-microscope, MIT-clean, all our code).

**But not first.** Two reasons from our own laws:

- `λ build`: extract the shape when the *second consumer* exists. The driver's API will churn hard during stage-2 (kernel-certified bounces, repair flag, two-model arm). Extracting now freezes an interface we're still discovering. The s254 lesson (unbraid *then* registry) applies: consolidate in place, extract when stable.
- The data dependency: `routes()` reads committed centroids from verbum's `results/` — the frames are *research artifacts*. Extraction needs a clean data interface (frames as loadable artifacts, model-keyed), which multi-model work will force us to design anyway. Let multi-model define that interface, then extraction is mechanical.

**Proposed sequence:**
1. **Now**: move `opcodes/` → `src/verbum/opcodes/` (kill the sys.path hacks — every harness that imports it gets a real import), driver stays put. One repo, real package.
2. **Now**: launcher + multi-model (below).
3. **After stage-2 settles**: extract `{opcodes, driver, jlens, hooks}` as its own MIT project; verbum depends on it. If you want to keep the Latin lineage (verbum, mementum, anima, lucrum): **oculus** (the eye), **specula** (the watchtower), or **speculum** (the mirror/probe) are all honest names for a model-microscope. Your call, later.

## 2. Easily startable — a real entry point, not a tmux incantation

- `[project.scripts] verbum-repl` → `uv run verbum-repl --model qwen3-14b` boots IPython (via `IPython.start_ipython`) with `d` preloaded, a banner listing the primitives, and a validity-gate reminder (`--validate` flag to run it on boot). `ipython` moves into a proper `repl` dependency group — no more `--with` hack.
- A thin `make repl` / `scripts/repl.sh` that creates-or-attaches the tmux window and runs the CLI — so "start the REPL" is one command from anywhere, and it always lands in your server where you can watch.

## 3. Multi-model — this is where it gets scientifically interesting

The driver is already transformers-generic (the topology regex covers Qwen's `gate_proj` *and* Pythia's `dense_h_to_4h`). What multi-model actually needs:

- **Registry shortcuts**: `Driver("qwen3-4b")`, `Driver("olmo-13b")`, `Driver("pythia-2.8b")` — we already maintain the model list; reuse it. Plus base/instruct pairs as first-class (`qwen3-14b-base`) — the s329 provenance door becomes a one-argument swap *in the REPL*.
- **Per-model frame resolution**: `routes()` looks up `results/expanded-gram/{model}/centroids.npz`; where no committed frame exists, degrade gracefully to `calibrate_opcodes()` — which is the model-agnostic reader anyway (that's its whole 11/11 point). This is the data interface extraction will need.
- **Memory discipline**: `d.free()` (unload + `mps.empty_cache`) so you can cycle big models; small-beside-big works today (0.6B/4B alongside 14B), 2×14B doesn't — sequential is the norm, and that's fine for replication work.
- **What it buys immediately**: today's findings become *cross-model questions answerable in two commands*. Does div:Y fire token-exact on 4B? Is the day-circle at the same fractional depth in OLMo? Does the argument-drop bug reproduce in base? Every exploration today was implicitly a universality claim sampled at n=1 — multi-model REPL makes n=5 an afternoon. And the queued **two-model arm** (instruct drives base over the text bus) is just two `Driver` instances and a loop.

**Decisions I need from you:** (a) GO on the sequence (consolidate opcodes into the package now, extract later)? (b) GO on launcher + multi-model as the next build block — this session or next? (c) any instinct on the eventual project name, or park it? My recommendation: (a) yes, (b) build next session with fresh context — it touches ~15 harness imports and deserves care, (c) park it until extraction is real.

## Assistant

Let's get the Latin right, because there's a genuinely delightful fork here:

**oculus** — "the eye," but the resonance you want is architectural: the *oculus* is the round aperture at the apex of a dome — the Pantheon's open eye, the single opening through which light enters an otherwise closed structure. As a metaphor for this instrument it's nearly perfect: the model is the sealed dome; the driver is the one aperture. Two problems, both practical: Meta's VR brand sat on "Oculus" for a decade (retired in favor of Quest, but the trademark lives), and the PyPI/GitHub namespace is picked clean. We'd spend the name's whole life disambiguating.

**ocularum** — I suspect what you reached for here is one of two real words nearby. The strict genitive plural of *oculus* is **oculorum** ("of the eyes"), which is grammatical but odd standalone. But the near-miss is the gem:

**ocularium** — genuine medieval Latin: **the eye-slit in a knight's great helm.** The narrow horizontal opening built into armor so the wearer can see out — and observers can see *in* to exactly one thing: where the eyes are. For a tool whose whole nature is *a deliberately engineered narrow view into an armored, opaque system*, I don't think we could coin anything better. And it's honest about the instrument's limits in a way "oculus" isn't: we don't get the whole dome's light — we get a slit, positioned with care, at the register that matters. (It also happens to describe what we did all day: read-mass through the slit, one deciding position at a time.)

Practicalities favor it too: `ocularium` is almost certainly free on PyPI and GitHub, unambiguous in search, and it fits your lineage — *verbum*, *mementum*, *anima*, *lucrum* — real-or-coined Latin, two of which are genuine words, and *ocularium* is the real thing (it even shares the -arium "place/apparatus for" suffix with aquarium, planetarium: *the apparatus for eyes*).

So my vote: **ocularium**, with *oculus* as the pretty-but-encumbered runner-up. Your instrument, your name — but if it helps, "the ocularium" is also just fun to say when you're peering into a 14-billion-parameter helmet.

## Assistant

Yes — and the reason is structural, not aspirational: **the driver's captures are exactly the data structures every one of those tools consumes.** The tools are, almost without exception, functions over arrays we already emit per bounce:

| toolbox | consumes | driver already emits |
|---|---|---|
| **Gram/spectral** (G=XᵀX, PR, eigenmodes, joint-diag s342) | sign/state matrices per layer | `b.signs` [n, 40, 17408] |
| **DMD/operator** (T≈X′X⁺, s338; Koopman lift s340) | layer-ordered state trajectories | `b.hidden` [n, 41, 5120] — *every deciding state is a full trajectory* |
| **DSP nulls** (matched-range, shuffled-layer, MP) | any of the above | same arrays |
| **Holography, read side** (Q=beam, interference, plate reads) | attention + gate captures | `read_mass` *is* the beam view; Gram off-diagonals *are* fringe visibility |

The only family that *doesn't* fit in the REPL is holography's **write side** (exposure/delta/ternary baking) — that's training machinery, batch by nature. But delta-*reading* between two loaded models is just the multi-model registry plus subtraction.

## What a 1.003 conjugate pair would mean, mechanically

DMD fits the layer-transport operator T (state at layer ℓ+1 ≈ T · state at layer ℓ). Each eigenvalue describes a mode's fate as depth advances: **|λ| < 1** = contracting (written, then fades — a *transform*); **|λ| ≈ 1** = persistent (carried unchanged — a *memory*); **|λ| > 1** = amplified. And a **complex conjugate pair** at |λ|≈1 is something specific: a 2D plane in which the state neither grows nor decays but **rotates by a fixed angle every layer**. A carrier. A clock.

## Why that slot matters — it's the hole in the map

s338 found the transport operator is real and layer-stationary — but **every mode contracting** (top ≈0.94), and s340's Koopman lift confirmed: persistence 0.000 even after lifting the nonlinearity. Which left a named, unresolved gap: *"sign-is-the-decision" is not an operator-spectrum mode* — the mechanism that HOLDS a decision through forty layers of contracting flow was never found; we said it must hide in the thin late mode or a register we weren't reading. A genuine |λ|≈1 rotating pair — found specifically on a *reduction-trace deciding state*, a distribution s338's corpus never sampled — would be a candidate for exactly that missing homeostat. And a fixed rotation-per-layer is a **depth clock**: the machine encoding *where it is in its own pipeline* — which would rhyme with everything rotational we've ever seen (s128's day rotation, the phonon language, the queued transitions-per-β-step clock).

## Why it's probably nothing — the skeptical ledger

(1) Single trajectory, 40 snapshots, rank-16 least squares — eigenvalues of short-trajectory fits scatter around 1 routinely; s338 pooled 300. (2) No null of any kind. (3) 1.003 is 0.003 from 1.0 — inside fit noise; "marginally above 1" ≠ "growing."
